- **A definition that merges a schema which merges it back in, taken as the root schema with no data, gives `{}` where it gave `undefined`.** With `Pet = { type: 'object', properties: { petType: { type: 'string' } }, if: { properties: { petType: { const: 'cat' } } }, then: { $ref: Cat } }`, `Cat = { allOf: [{ $ref: Pet }, { type: 'object', properties: { hunts: { type: 'boolean' } } }] }` and a root `$ref` to `Cat`: before `undefined`, after `{}`.
- **Under `allOf: 'populateDefaults'`, the option an `allOf` entry chooses gives its defaults over an earlier entry's, nested as at the root.** With `o: { type: 'object', allOf: [{ properties: { c: { type: 'string', default: 'm0' } } }, { oneOf: [{ properties: { c: { type: 'string', default: 'opt' } } }, { properties: { z: { type: 'number' } } }] }] }`: before `{ o: { c: 'm0' } }`, after `{ o: { c: 'opt' } }`. The same object at the root already gave `{ c: 'opt' }`, and the form renders `c` from the chosen option. A field's schemas are merged in a fixed order (its own, its `$ref`, its `allOf`, the `then`/`else`, `dependencies`, patterns, then the option), and a later one's `default` wins, as a matching `then`'s already does. Where the option declares no default for the key, the earlier entry's still applies. In such shapes the option's keys can also come first in the defaults object; the values are the same.
- **Under `allOf: 'skipDefaults'`, a deep `customMergeAllOf` no longer fills a property's `allOf` defaults.** Each field's defaults are computed from the field as the schema declares it, so a merger that also flattens the `allOf` of the properties inside the `allOf` it merges, such as `createDeepAllOfMerge` from `@x0k/json-schema-merge`, no longer exempts those properties from `skipDefaults`. With `animalInfo: { properties: { animal: { default: 'Cat' } }, allOf: [{ if: { properties: { animal: { const: 'Cat' } } }, then: { properties: { food: { default: 'meat' } } } }] }` and an `allOf` beside it at the root: before `{ animalInfo: { animal: 'Cat', food: 'meat' } }`, after `{ animalInfo: { animal: 'Cat' } }`, as without that merger. `allOf: 'populateDefaults'` fills it.
- **A root `dependencies` entry counts a default as its property's value whatever else the form data holds**, as a nested one already did. With `properties: { animal: { default: 'Cat' }, other: { type: 'string' } }` and `dependencies: { animal: { properties: { food: { default: 'meat' } } } }`, for the form data `{ other: 'x' }`: before `{ animal: 'Cat', other: 'x' }`, after `{ animal: 'Cat', other: 'x', food: 'meat' }`.

These stay as they were:

- A recursion through an `anyOf`/`oneOf` option stops where the same recursion written as a property does, however deep the data goes below it, also when the property reaches the option list through a `$ref` of its own. With `node = { properties: { name: { default: 'n' }, c: { anyOf: [{ $ref: node }, { type: 'null' }] } } }`, a root `$ref` to `node` and the data `{ c: { c: { c: {} } } }`: `{ name: 'n', c: { name: 'n', c: { c: {} } } }`.
- `getDefaultFormState(pl)` fills three levels, and `getDefaultFormState({ $ref: pl })` two.
- A default that satisfies an `if`, or a choice of `anyOf`/`oneOf` option, can switch the branch on the next call, so calling `getDefaultFormState()` on its own result can add more defaults. With `{ properties: { a: { default: 'x' } }, if: { required: ['a'] }, then: { properties: { b: { default: 'b' } } } }`: the first call gives `{ a: 'x' }`, and the second `{ a: 'x', b: 'b' }`.

#### The root schema is treated as immutable BREAKING CHANGE

The graph is parsed once per root schema object and kept while that object lives, and the plans and resolved schemas are kept against it.
A change made to the root schema in place is therefore not seen, not even by a new `createSchemaUtils()` or by `Form` rendering it again.
Before, the next `retrieveSchema()` read the schema as it then stood.
To change a schema, pass a new object, as you would for any other React prop:

```ts
const schema = { type: 'object', properties: { color: { type: 'string', enum: ['red'] } } };
createSchemaUtils({ validator }, schema).retrieveSchema(schema);
schema.properties.color.enum = ['red', 'green'];
createSchemaUtils({ validator }, schema).retrieveSchema(schema).properties.color.enum;
// Before: ['red', 'green']
// After: ['red'], since `schema` is the object already parsed

const changed = { ...schema, properties: { color: { type: 'string', enum: ['red', 'green'] } } };
createSchemaUtils({ validator }, changed).retrieveSchema(changed).properties.color.enum;
// Before and after: ['red', 'green']
```

A schema you hand `retrieveSchema()` that the root does not hold, such as one a custom field builds from scratch, is parsed once per content and kept for as long as the root, and so is one you hand any other schema utility that resolves it, such as `getDefaultFormState()`, `isSelect()` or `getClosestMatchingOption()`.
Calls handed the same content share its result, so do not change such a schema once handed in either.
Build it from the schema rather than from form data: one built from form data keeps an entry for each distinct value until the root changes.

#### `createSchemaUtils()` no longer rewrites the root's `$ref`s BREAKING CHANGE

`createSchemaUtils()` used to rewrite every `$ref` of a draft 2020-12 root against the root's `$id` and keep that copy as its root schema.
It now keeps the root schema it is given, and each `$ref` is resolved once, against the `$id`s around it, where it is looked up ([#5529](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5529)).
`getRootSchema()`, and with it a form's `registry.rootSchema`, is the schema you passed:

```ts
const schema = {
  $schema: 'https://json-schema.org/draft/2020-12/schema',
  $id: 'https://example.com/root.json',
  $defs: { s: { type: 'string' } },
  type: 'object',
  properties: { a: { $ref: '#/$defs/s' } },
};
const schemaUtils = createSchemaUtils({ validator }, schema);
schemaUtils.getRootSchema().properties.a;
// Before: { $ref: 'https://example.com/root.json#/$defs/s' }
// After: { $ref: '#/$defs/s' }, since getRootSchema() returns `schema` itself
```

- A `ui:definitions` entry is keyed by the `$ref` as the schema writes it. With the schema above, `'ui:definitions': { '#/$defs/s': { 'ui:widget': 'textarea' } }` renders `root_a` as a textarea. Before, it rendered a text input, and only an entry keyed `https://example.com/root.json#/$defs/s` applied. Rename such keys to the `$ref` as written.
- A `$ref` the rewrite left as written, because a validator would resolve the rewritten one again to somewhere else, resolves against the resource it sits in wherever it is looked up. Under `$id: 'schemas/root.json'`, with `$defs: { x: { type: 'number' }, child: { $id: 'nested/child.json', $defs: { x: { type: 'string' } }, additionalProperties: { $ref: '#/$defs/x' } } }`: before, `schemaUtils.retrieveSchema()` of the `additionalProperties` that `getRootSchema()` holds gave the root's `{ type: 'number' }`. After, it gives the child's `{ type: 'string' }`.
- `makeAllReferencesAbsolute()` is removed. A schema it rewrote before it was handed to `Form` or `createSchemaUtils()` can be handed over as it is.

#### Deep imports and precompiled validators BREAKING CHANGE

- `@rjsf/utils`' `lib/findSchemaDefinition.js` keeps the deprecated `findSchemaDefinition()` but no longer exports `makeAllReferencesAbsolute()`, which is removed, since [`createSchemaUtils()` no longer rewrites the root's `$ref`s](#createschemautils-no-longer-rewrites-the-roots-refs-breaking-change).
- `@rjsf/utils`' `lib/schema/retrieveSchema.js` no longer exports `resolveAllReferences()`, and `lib/refOf.js` is removed.
- `@rjsf/utils`' `lib/getOptionMatchingSimpleDiscriminator.js` is removed with `getOptionMatchingSimpleDiscriminator()`.
- `@rjsf/utils`' `lib/schemaHasNestedConditional.js` is removed with `schemaHasNestedConditional()`.
- `@rjsf/utils`' `lib/parser/schemaParser.js` exports the deprecated `schemaParser()` by name only, no longer as its default: import `getValidationSchemas()` from `@rjsf/utils` in its place.
- The `retrieveSchema()` helpers in that module, such as `retrieveSchemaInternal()`, `resolveSchema()` and `resolveDependencies()`, are no longer exported: see [validators are handed pointers into the root schema](#validators-are-handed-pointers-into-the-root-schema-breaking-change).
- `computeDefaults()`, `getObjectDefaults()`, `getArrayDefaults()` and `getDefaultBasedOnSchemaType()` in `lib/schema/getDefaultFormState.js` no longer take a `_recurseList`. Each call starts the recursion afresh at the schema it is given, so `computeDefaults()` on a recursive `$ref` fills it one level further: before `{ name: '' }`, after `{ name: '', children: { name: '' } }`.
- `getClosestMatchingOption()` and `omitExtraData()` resolve only an option's own `$ref` and leave the ones below it to the validator.
- `getClosestMatchingOption()` scores a property written as a `$ref` as the definition it names, as far as the form data reaches, whether or not the `$ref` is recursive, resolving each `$ref` it follows in the resource of the declaration holding it. With `Node = { type: 'object', properties: { next: { $ref: Node } } }`, the options `[{ type: 'object', properties: { a: { $ref: Node } } }, { type: 'object', properties: { a: { type: 'object', properties: { next: { type: 'object' }, z: { type: 'string' } } } } }]` and the data `{ a: { next: { next: {} } } }`: before, it overflowed the stack. After, it picks the first option. `calculateIndexScore()` called directly scores a `$ref` property the same way, so one naming an object gets the point for an object value that it gets written in place, and the `allOf` of the definition is not merged: with `O = { type: 'object', properties: { x: { type: 'string' } } }`, `calculateIndexScore(context, rootSchema, { properties: { a: { $ref: O } } }, { a: { x: 'y' } })` gives 1 before and 2 after.
- Precompiled validator code must be regenerated; see [Validators are handed pointers into the root schema](#validators-are-handed-pointers-into-the-root-schema-breaking-change).
- `getDefaultFormState()` scores a field's options as the schema declares them, so a parent's `customMergeAllOf` no longer reaches the option its children choose for their defaults.

#### Validators are handed pointers into the root schema BREAKING CHANGE

The form checks a condition or an option by handing the validator a `$ref` to where it sits in the root schema, rather than a copy of it.
The `rootSchema` passed beside it is what the `$ref` resolves against, so a subschema means what JSON Schema says it means wherever the form reads it, and the schemas a precompiled validator compiles are named by the schema graph rather than enumerated.

**A `ValidatorType` of your own has to resolve the `$ref`.**
`isValid(schema, formData, rootSchema)` is handed `{ $ref: '#/<pointer>' }` for each `if` a field's resolution checks, the property an `anyOf`/`oneOf` option names as its discriminator, an option scoring has no `required` to drop from, the option `MultiSchemaField` keeps after a parent declines a switch, and, inside `{ type: 'object', properties: { <key>: { $ref } } }`, the property a dependency's `oneOf` option is matched on.
A property an option declares more than once where it applies whatever the data, as `allOf: [{ $ref: Base }, { properties: { petType: { const: 'cat' } } }]` does when `Base` declares `petType` too, is handed as an `allOf` of a `$ref` to each declaration.
With this schema, `retrieveSchema()` for the data `{ a: 'x' }` makes one call:

```ts
const schema: RJSFSchema = {
  type: 'object',
  properties: { a: { type: 'string' } },
  if: { properties: { a: { const: 'x' } }, required: ['a'] },
  then: { properties: { extra: { type: 'string' } } },
};
// before: isValid({ properties: { a: { const: 'x' } }, required: ['a'] }, { a: 'x' }, schema)
// after:  isValid({ $ref: '#/if' }, { a: 'x' }, schema)
```

`@rjsf/validator-ajv8`, `@rjsf/validator-ata` and `@rjsf/validator-cfworker` register the root under its `$id` less any fragment, or under `ROOT_SCHEMA_PREFIX` when that leaves nothing, as `rootSchemaIdOf()` returns it, and prefix a local `$ref` with that id through `withRootIdRefPrefix(schema, rootSchema)`; `@rjsf/validator-ajv8` uses the id as its `uriResolver` normalizes it, `http://example.com/a%20b.json` for `http://example.com/a b.json` and `http://example.com/a.json` for `http://example.com:80/a.json`, which is what Ajv looks a `$ref` up by.
A validator that ignores `rootSchema` finds nothing for the `$ref`, so every condition and option it checks fails.

**A root carrying an `$id`.**
`@rjsf/validator-ajv8` and `@rjsf/validator-ata` named the root by `ROOT_SCHEMA_PREFIX` in every local `$ref`, which a root with an `$id` is not registered under, so a condition holding such a `$ref` never held.
With `{ $id: 'http://example.com/root.json', definitions: { I: { properties: { k: { const: 'x' } } } }, properties: { k: { type: 'string', default: 'x' } }, if: { $ref: '#/definitions/I' }, then: { properties: { t: { type: 'string', default: 't' } } }, else: { properties: { e: { type: 'string', default: 'e' } } } }`, `getDefaultFormState()` gives `{ k: 'x', t: 't' }` where it gave `{ k: 'x', e: 'e' }`.
An `$id` ending in an empty fragment, as `http://example.com/root.json#` and the `$id`s of the draft-06 and draft-07 meta-schemas do, names the same root, so `rootSchemaIdOf()` leaves that `#` out of the prefix rather than writing `http://example.com/root.json##/if`.
`@rjsf/validator-ajv8` registers such a root under its `$id` without the `#` too, which is how Ajv keys it, so a root edited under the same `$id` replaces the one before it: with a `customizeValidator()` instance that has validated against `{ $id: 'http://example.com/root.json#', type: 'object', properties: { k: { type: 'string', default: 'x' } }, if: { properties: { k: { const: 'x' } } }, then: { properties: { t: { type: 'string', default: 't' } } }, else: { properties: { e: { type: 'string', default: 'e' } } } }`, the same root with a `title` added gives the defaults `{ k: 'x', t: 't' }` where it gave `{ k: 'x', e: 'e' }` and logged `schema with key or id "http://example.com/root.json" already exists`.

`@rjsf/validator-cfworker` registered a root carrying an `$id` under `ROOT_SCHEMA_PREFIX` in its place, so the `$ref`s of a 2020-12 root with an `$id`, which resolve against that `$id`, resolved to nothing, and a condition holding one never held.
With `{ $schema: 'https://json-schema.org/draft/2020-12/schema', $id: 'https://example.com/root.json', $defs: { isB: { properties: { k: { const: 'b' } }, required: ['k'] } }, type: 'object', properties: { k: { type: 'string' } }, if: { $ref: '#/$defs/isB' }, then: { properties: { t: { type: 'string', default: 'THEN' } } }, else: { properties: { e: { type: 'string', default: 'ELSE' } } } }` and the data `{ k: 'b' }`, `getDefaultFormState()` gives `{ k: 'b', t: 'THEN' }` where it gave `{ k: 'b', e: 'ELSE' }`.
Options read from that root match the same way: with `$defs: { isA: { type: 'object', properties: { k: { const: 'a' } }, required: ['k'] }, isB: { type: 'object', properties: { k: { const: 'b' } }, required: ['k'] } }` and `oneOf: [{ $ref: '#/$defs/isA' }, { $ref: '#/$defs/isB' }]` in such a root, `getFirstMatchingOption({ k: 'b' }, schemaUtils.getRootSchema().oneOf)` gives `1` where it gave `0`.

**One validator, several roots.**
A validator caches what it compiles for a schema, and `{ $ref: '#/definitions/X' }` means something else under every root.
`isValid({ $ref: '#/definitions/X' }, 's', { definitions: { X: { type: 'number' } } })` after the same call with a root whose `X` is a `string` gives `false` where it gave `true`: replacing the root drops what was compiled against it.

**Option scoring.**
`getFirstMatchingOption()` and `getClosestMatchingOption()` match an option as JSON Schema reads it, and check in JavaScript that the data holds a key the option declares: a key named by its `properties` or `patternProperties`, or by those of its `$ref` target, its `allOf` entries, its `then`/`else` and its `dependencies` schemas.
Where something in that scope declares `properties`, scoring drops every `required` in the scope, and every `dependencies` entry listing names, so a user who has yet to fill in a required key is still matched to the option; before, it dropped only the option's own `required`, and only when the option itself declared `properties`.
An option whose scope declares no `properties`, as in the "at least one of" pattern, is matched by its `required` as before:

```ts
const rootSchema: RJSFSchema = {
  type: 'object',
  properties: { email: { type: 'string' }, phone: { type: 'string' } },
  oneOf: [{ title: 'By email', required: ['email'] }, { title: 'By phone', required: ['phone'] }],
};
getFirstMatchingOption(context, { phone: '1' }, rootSchema.oneOf, rootSchema);
// before and after: 1
```

An option written as a `$ref`, or composed with `allOf`, is now matched as one written out in place is:

```ts
const rootSchema: RJSFSchema = {
  definitions: {
    A: { type: 'object', properties: { a: { type: 'number' } } },
    B: { type: 'object', properties: { b: { type: 'number' } } },
    Base: { type: 'object', properties: { id: { type: 'string' } }, required: ['id'] },
  },
};
getFirstMatchingOption(context, { b: 1 }, [{ $ref: '#/definitions/A' }, { $ref: '#/definitions/B' }], rootSchema);
// before: 0, the first option matching any object; after: 1
getFirstMatchingOption(
  context,
  { kind: 'x' },
  [
    { type: 'object', properties: { kind: { const: 'y' } } },
    { allOf: [{ $ref: '#/definitions/Base' }], properties: { kind: { const: 'x' } } },
  ],
  rootSchema,
);
// before: 0, since Base's `required` failed the second option; after: 1
```

Where an option's own `then` overrides a keyword when the form renders it, scoring now holds the data to both, as validating the whole form on submit already did.
When `transformChange` rewrites an option switch, `MultiSchemaField` checks the data against the option it keeps, read the same way.
`getOptionMatchingSimpleDiscriminator()` and `relaxOptionsForScoring()` are removed, since option scoring no longer calls them; see [Changes to existing utility functions](#changes-to-existing-utility-functions).

**Dependency `oneOf` options.**
A `dependencies` schema's `oneOf` option is applied when the property the dependency names fits what the option declares for it, and that declaration is now found in the same scope: the option's own `properties`, or those of its `$ref` target, its `allOf` entries, its `then`/`else` and its `dependencies` schemas.
Before, only its own `properties` or its `$ref` target's counted, so an option declaring the property only in an `allOf` entry never applied:

```ts
const schema: RJSFSchema = {
  type: 'object',
  properties: { k: { type: 'string', enum: ['a', 'b'] } },
  dependencies: {
    k: {
      oneOf: [
        { allOf: [{ properties: { k: { enum: ['a'] } } }], properties: { ka: { type: 'string', default: 'A' } } },
        { allOf: [{ properties: { k: { enum: ['b'] } } }], properties: { kb: { type: 'string', default: 'B' } } },
      ],
    },
  },
};
getDefaultFormState(context, { schema, rootSchema: schema, formData: { k: 'b' } });
// before: { k: 'b' }, the form shows `k` alone and warns "ignoring oneOf in dependencies"
// after:  { k: 'b', kb: 'B' }, the form shows `k` and `kb`
```

The same reading can make a second option apply where one did, and the dependency then applies neither, since exactly one option has to match:

```ts
const schema: RJSFSchema = {
  type: 'object',
  properties: { k: { type: 'string', enum: ['a', 'b'] } },
  dependencies: {
    k: {
      oneOf: [
        { allOf: [{ properties: { k: { enum: ['a', 'b'] } } }], properties: { ka: { type: 'string', default: 'A' } } },
        { properties: { k: { enum: ['b'] }, kb: { type: 'string', default: 'B' } } },
      ],
    },
  },
};
getDefaultFormState(context, { schema, rootSchema: schema, formData: { k: 'b' } });
// before: { k: 'b', kb: 'B' }, the form shows `k` and `kb`
// after:  { k: 'b' }, the form shows `k` alone and warns "ignoring oneOf in dependencies"
```

**Precompiled validators.**
Regenerate precompiled validator code with `compileSchemaValidators()` or `compileSchemaValidatorsCode()`: code generated by an earlier version throws `No precompiled validator function was found`.
The new `getValidationSchemas(rootSchema)` lists what is compiled: the root, a `$ref` to each `if` and option, to the property each option declares under any `discriminator.propertyName` in the root, the copy of an option scoring derives where it drops a `required`, each dependency condition, and, where an option is a boolean, `{}`, the schema `SchemaField` retrieves it as, with `{ not: {} }`, the schema a `false` option reads as.
`schemaParser()` is deprecated: it is `getValidationSchemas()` under its 6.x name, so it returns this list; call `getValidationSchemas()` in its place.
The list depends on the root alone and grows with its size, so schemas that failed to compile or failed in the form now work:

| Schema                                                                                                                                                                                                              | 6.11.0                                                                             | 7.0.0                                                                             |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `definitions.node` recursing through `u: { anyOf: [{ $ref: node }, { type: 'string' }] }`, used as `v: { oneOf: [{ $ref: node }, { type: 'number' }] }`                                                             | the compile throws `can't resolve reference #/definitions/node`                    | compiles; `v` matches `{ n: 'a', u: { n: 'b' } }` to option 0 and `5` to option 1 |
| a root with `dependencies: { a: ['b'] }` and `oneOf: [{ $ref: '#' }, { properties: { c } }]` ([#5452](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5452) c)                                            | the compile overflows the stack                                                    | compiles                                                                          |
| an option with `properties: { id }` and `patternProperties: { '^s_': ..., '^s_n': ... }`, data `{ id: 'a', s_na: 'xx' }` ([#5454](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5454))                  | scoring the retrieved options throws `No precompiled validator function was found` | option 0                                                                          |
| an option `{ $id: 'myOption', properties: { id }, additionalProperties: { type: 'string' } }`, data `{ id: 'a', extra: 'x' }` ([#5455](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5455))             | the compile throws `schema with key or id "myOption" already exists`               | option 0                                                                          |
| `s: { allOf: [{ discriminator: { propertyName: 'kind' } }], oneOf: [A, B] }`, or `s: { discriminator, allOf: [{ oneOf: [A, B] }] }`, where `A` and `B` declare `kind` as `const` `'a'` and `'b'`, data `{ b: 'q' }` | scoring the options throws `No precompiled validator function was found`           | option 1                                                                          |

A schema a merge makes has no place in the root and is not compiled, as in 6.11.0: an option a `customMergeAllOf` rewrites, or one made by merging the `oneOf`s of two `patternProperties` matching the same key, throws `No precompiled validator function was found` when the form scores it.
The error names the subschema when the schema it has no function for is a `$ref`.
A subschema the root holds in several places, as a schema module that reuses one object holds it, is compiled at each place, so code compiled from the module serves an equal copy of it, such as its JSON, and the other way round, as in 6.11.0.

`@rjsf/validator-ata` compiles each schema as a document of its own, and `ata-validator` resolves a `$ref` only to an entry of the `definitions` or `$defs` in it.
So each document carries what it reaches of the root: every subschema of the root it names through a `$ref`, such as a definition, a property, an option or the root itself, as an entry of its own in one container, the `$ref` renamed to it, since `ata-validator` compiles nothing for a document holding both `definitions` and `$defs`, and does not percent-decode a `$ref` such as `#/definitions/c%20d`.
A `$ref` beside other keywords is compiled as an `allOf` entry beside them, as 2019-09, 2020-12 and Ajv read it, since `ata-validator` compiles no `$ref` beside keywords that assert something.
A property that refers back to itself, `tree: { type: 'object', properties: { name: { type: 'string' }, kids: { type: 'array', items: { $ref: '#/properties/tree' } } } }`, now validates, reporting `.tree.kids.0.kids.0.name` for `{ tree: { kids: [{ kids: [{ name: 5 }] }] } }`, where 6.11.0 overflowed the stack.
A `$ref` that names nothing in the root, or one into another document, leaves `ata-validator` nothing to compile, which throws `was not compiled to a standalone validator` when the form validates against it.
`ata-validator` compiles no function for a schema holding only annotations either, so each schema without a `not` or a `$ref` is compiled with `not: { not: {} }`, which always holds: a precompiled validator for the root `{ title: 'Anything' }` returns `true` from `isValid()` where it threw `was not compiled to a standalone validator`.

**Deep imports.**
`lib/schema/retrieveSchema.js` no longer exports `retrieveSchemaInternal()`, `resolveSchema()`, `resolveReference()`, `resolveCondition()`, `resolveAnyOrOneOfSchemas()`, `resolveDependencies()`, `processDependencies()`, `withDependentProperties()`, `withDependentSchema()`, `withExactlyOneSubschema()` or `getAllPermutationsOfXxxOf()`, which only expanded every branch for `schemaParser()`.
`lib/combinationsOf.js` and `lib/parser/ParserValidator.js` are removed, as are `JUNK_OPTION` from `lib/schema/getClosestMatchingOption.js` and `lib/qualifyRootRefs.js` from `@rjsf/validator-ajv8`, whose job `withRootIdRefPrefix(schema, rootSchema)` does.
`lib/parser/schemaParser.js` exports `schemaParser()` by name only, no longer as its default: replace `import schemaParser from '@rjsf/utils/lib/parser/schemaParser.js'` with `import { getValidationSchemas } from '@rjsf/utils'`.
`JUNK_OPTION_ID` is removed too: nothing read it any more.

### Option selection and cycle expansion are form state

The `anyOf`/`oneOf` option a field shows and which collapsed `$ref` cycles are expanded are kept by the form, by field path, rather than by the `AnyOfField`/`OneOfField` and `CyclicSchemaField` components.
They survive the field unmounting and mounting again and follow the moves the form makes, of array items and of additional properties renamed or removed.
The sections below list what changes; "before" is what 6.11.0 gives.

#### The selected option no longer follows the data while you type BREAKING CHANGE

With no selection, a field shows the option `getClosestMatchingOption()` picks for its data, as before.
A selection, the user's or that pick once an edit is made inside the option, holds for the data the form last rendered under it and for the data the form's own changes inside the option leave at the field, whatever those edits made of it, so a switch or an edit `transformChange` refuses keeps it too.
Other data, such as `setFieldValue()`'s, a reset, a `transformChange` that rewrites a switch to data the chosen option does not fit, or an edit to a property the object declares beside its options that the options can disagree about, one an option names under `properties`, such as with a `const` that tells the options apart, or under `required`, or any such property where an option carries a keyword that reads the object whole, such as `minProperties`, `not` or `additionalProperties`, is shown under the option `getClosestMatchingOption()` picks for it, the selection winning a tie, as on 6.11.0, even when it is the data the field showed before the switch.
An edit to a property every option is silent about, where each carries only `type`, annotations such as `title`, and `properties` and `required` that leave the property out, holds the option shown, as an edit inside the option does: every option says the same of that property, so the edit cannot be a choice between them.
That pick does not replace the selection, and the selection is kept by field path, so it outlives the data it was made for.
A field whose data holds a value for the `discriminator.propertyName` its schema declares is always shown under the option that value names, as before: the discriminator is the selector, so an edit to it is a switch.

- With `o: { anyOf: [{ title: 'A', type: 'object', properties: { a: { type: 'string', default: 'd' } } }, { title: 'B', type: 'object', properties: { a: { type: 'string' }, b: { type: 'string' } } }] }`, select `B`, clear `root_o_a` and type `d`. Before: the selector went back to `A`, whose `default` the data now matched, and `root_o_b` disappeared. After: it stays on `B`, with `root_o_b`.
- With `{ type: 'object', properties: { name: { type: 'string' } }, oneOf: [{ title: 'A', properties: { a: { type: 'string' } } }, { title: 'B', properties: { a: { type: 'string', maxLength: 2 }, b: { type: 'string' } }, required: ['b'] }] }`, select `B`, type `z` into `root_b`, `abc` into `root_a` and then `n` into `root_name`. Before: the selector went to `A` at `abc`, which `B` rejects, and `root_b` disappeared; with `omitExtraData` and `liveOmit`, `b: 'z'` was dropped there. After: it stays on `B` through both edits, with `{ a: 'abc', b: 'z', name: 'n' }`.
- With `{ type: 'object', properties: { a: { type: 'string' }, b: { type: 'string' } }, oneOf: [{ title: 'A', required: ['a'] }, { title: 'B', required: ['b'] }] }` and `{}`, type `x` into `root_b`: the selector goes to `B`, before and after, since `B`'s `required` names `b`. So does `anyOf: [{ title: 'Few', maxProperties: 1 }, { title: 'Many', minProperties: 2 }]` with `{ a: 'x' }` when `y` is typed into `root_b`: `Many`, before and after.
- A condition that swaps the option list a field offers starts the choice over. With `kind: { enum: ['a', 'b'], default: 'a' }`, `if: { properties: { kind: { const: 'a' } } }`, `then: { properties: { x: { anyOf: [A1, A2] } } }` and `else: { properties: { x: { anyOf: [B1, B2] } } }`, select `A2`, then `b` for `kind`. Before: `B2`, the index carried over. After: `B1`, the option the data matches; selecting `a` again shows `A2` before and after.
- A field that mounts again shows the option selected before. With the `o` above, select `B`, then render the form with `o: { 'ui:field': Nothing, 'ui:fieldReplacesAnyOrOneOf': true }` and again without it. Before: `A`. After: `B`.
- Data `setFieldValue()` sets that every option fits shows the selection, rather than the option an earlier call matched. With the root `oneOf: [{ title: 'A', type: 'object', properties: { a: { type: 'string' } } }, { title: 'B', type: 'object', properties: { b: { type: 'string' } } }]`, select `B` and type `y` into `root_b`; `setFieldValue('', { a: 'x' })` then shows `A` before and after, and `setFieldValue('', {})` shows, before, `A`, and after, `B`.
- A selection outlives the array item it was made for. With `{ type: 'array', items: ...that oneOf }` and `[{}, {}]`, select `B` on the second item; `setFieldValue('', [{}])` and later `setFieldValue('', [{}, {}])`. Before: the new second item shows `A`. After: `B`, the selection made at that index, which the new item's data fits. An item a field removes or moves takes its selection with it.

`AnyOfField` and `OneOfField` read and write the selection with `useOptionSelection()`, which `@rjsf/core` exports, and `SchemaField` reads it to stub an object's extra keys with the selected option (below).
A custom `AnyOfField` or `OneOfField` that keeps the selection in its own state shows one option while the object beside it is stubbed for another, so read and write it through the hook instead; the `options` prop stays:

```tsx
import { useOptionSelection } from '@rjsf/core';

const { selectedOption, selectOption } = useOptionSelection(registry, fieldPath, options, formData, discriminator);
// on a pick: selectOption(index), then onChange(dataForTheNewOption, fieldPath)
```

#### An object's extra keys are stubbed once, with the selected option BREAKING CHANGE

An object field whose `anyOf`/`oneOf` options are rendered beside it stubs the extra keys of its data once, for itself and the selected option together.
A key the selected option declares is left to the option, which renders it, and the object takes the option's `additionalProperties`, `unevaluatedProperties` and `patternProperties` beside its own: a key either describes is described by both, and a key either forbids is forbidden.
The option's own field no longer stubs keys a second time.
Where the selected option offers options of its own, the field it renders beside them takes the extra keys in the object's place, for the object, the option and the option it selects together, since only that field knows which of its options declares a key.

- `additionalProperties: { type: 'object', properties: { host: { type: 'string' }, port: { type: 'number' } } }` beside `anyOf: [{ patternProperties: { '^x-': { type: 'string' } } }]`, with the data `{ db: { host: 'h', port: 1 } }` ([#5462](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5462)). Before: `db` rendered twice, as the object's `root_db_host` and `root_db_port` and as a second key row the option stubbed. After: once, with `root_db_host` and `root_db_port`.
- `properties: { name: { type: 'string' } }` beside `oneOf: [{ additionalProperties: { type: 'number' } }]`, with the data `{ name: 'n', k: 1 }`. Before: `root_name` rendered twice, the second time as a key row the option stubbed. After: once, with `k` as a number row and the add button beside it.
- `additionalProperties: true` beside `oneOf: [{ oneOf: [{ properties: { x: { type: 'string' } }, additionalProperties: { type: 'string' } }] }]`, with the data `{ x: 'a', extra: 'e' }`. Before: `x` and `extra` rendered twice, as the object's key rows and the nested option's, with an add button in each of the two object fields. After: once, in the nested option's field, with one add button.
- `additionalProperties: { type: 'string' }` beside `oneOf: [{ properties: { a: { type: 'string' } }, additionalProperties: false }]`, with the data `{ a: 'v', other: 'o' }`. Before: `root_a` rendered twice, once as a string row of the object, and `other` as a string row. After: `root_a` once, the option's, and `other` gets no field, since the option forbids it.
- `additionalProperties: { type: 'string' }` beside `anyOf: [{ properties: { count: { type: 'number' } } }, { properties: { count: { type: 'number' }, b: { type: 'string' } } }]`: add a property and rename `newKey` to `count` ([#5461](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5461)). Before: the data was `{ count: 'New Value' }`, the string the add button wrote for a key the object describes, shown in the option's number field and, on 6.11.0, in a second key row as well. After: the data is `{ count: 0 }`, seeded from the option's declaration, with `root_count` once. The option is the one `SchemaField` shows, so where a `ui:field` with `ui:fieldReplacesAnyOrOneOf` keeps the options from rendering, the key is an extra key and the rename gives `{ count: 'New Value' }`, before and after.
- `retrieveSchema()` takes the selected option as a new last argument, `selectedOption`. Without it, the extra keys are stubbed for the object alone, a key an option declares included, as on 6.11.0.
- `getAdditionalPropertyType()` reads the schema it is given as it stands and no longer takes a `rootSchema`: resolve the subschema first with `retrieveSchema(subSchema, formData, true)`, which resolves its options too, so options spelled as `$ref`s agree on a type as before.
- `getRenderedOptionSchema()` renders the option of an object without its `additionalProperties`, `patternProperties` and `unevaluatedProperties`.
- `ObjectField` renders the schema it is handed as it stands, where it retrieved it again for the data: `SchemaField` hands it the schema already retrieved, its extra keys stubbed with the selected option. A custom field that renders `ObjectField` itself with a schema it did not receive from `SchemaField`, such as a definition looked up in the root schema, retrieves it first, or the properties its `$ref`, `allOf` and conditions bring in, and the extra keys of the data, get no field: `<ObjectField {...props} schema={registry.schemaUtils.retrieveSchema(schema, formData)} />`. One that passes on the `schema` prop it received is unaffected.

#### `getClosestMatchingOption()` picks among the candidates BREAKING CHANGE

`getClosestMatchingOption()` returns an option the data matches whenever one does, and some option whenever there are options: a tie goes to the `selectedOption` only where the data matches it, or where no option matches the data, and to the first candidate otherwise.
A property holding an `anyOf`/`oneOf` of its own adds the score of the option picked among those, plus one where the data matches it, rather than that option's index.

```ts
getClosestMatchingOption(context, rootSchema, undefined, [{ type: 'string' }, { type: 'number' }]);
// Before: -1. After: 0
getClosestMatchingOption(context, rootSchema, undefined, [{ type: 'string' }, { type: 'number' }], 2);
// Before: 2, an index past the options. After: 0
```

A field opens on such a pick, so a field whose first option is `{ type: 'null', title: 'None' }` and whose data several other options match opens on one of those: with five definitions `A` to `E`, each `{ type: 'object', properties: { child: { oneOf: [{ type: 'null', title: 'None' }, ...a $ref to each of the four others] } } }`, the root `oneOf: [{ type: 'null', title: 'None' }, ...a $ref to each definition]` and the `formData` `{ child: { child: { child: null } } }`, before, the form opened on `None` and rendered the root selector alone; after, it opens on `A` and renders a selector at each of the four levels.

The pick is remembered per option list and data object, so a form whose definitions refer to each other through `oneOf` options matches each level of its data once ([#4990](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/4990)): rendering a form of five such definitions with the `formData` four levels deep validated 2124 times before, four times as many per level, and 122 times after, 35 more per level. Within one call a remembered pick is reused without looking at the data again, since a validator's `isValid()` must not change the data it checks (see [below](#a-validators-isvalid-must-leave-the-data-as-it-is-breaking-change)). A later call reuses it only while the data still holds what a copy kept with it holds, so data you mutate in place between calls is matched for what it holds now; that comparison costs time in proportion to the data below the option list, once per call. An object other than an array or a plain object, such as a `Date`, a `File` or a class instance, can't be copied, so a pick for data holding one is reused only within the call that made it: each call matches that data again, still once per level of it, and sees a change made in place since the last call.

#### A validator's `isValid()` must leave the data as it is BREAKING CHANGE

`ValidatorType.isValid()` must not change the data it is handed, at any depth, since `getClosestMatchingOption()` reuses a pick it made for some data during the same call without comparing the data again.
`@rjsf/validator-ajv8` validates a copy of the data in `isValid()` under Ajv's `useDefaults`, `coerceTypes` and `removeAdditional` options and with a keyword registered with `modifying: true`, where Ajv wrote defaults and coerced values into the form's data and deleted properties from it while the form checked a condition or an option; `validateFormData()` and `rawValidation()` still validate the data itself.
`@rjsf/validator-ata` already validated a copy, and `@rjsf/validator-cfworker` doesn't change the data.
A `ValidatorType` of your own whose engine fills in defaults, coerces values or removes properties as it validates has to hand it a copy of the data in `isValid()`.

```ts
const validator = customizeValidator({ ajvOptionsOverrides: { useDefaults: true } });
const schema: RJSFSchema = { type: 'object', properties: { flag: { type: 'string', default: 'on' } } };
const formData = {};
validator.isValid(schema, formData, schema);
// Before: formData is { flag: 'on' }. After: formData is {}, and isValid() still returns true
```

#### Switching from a `null` option restores the field's `default`

A field that held nothing, as after a `null` option, and switches to an option whose defaults are an object gets the defaults of the field with that option alone, so a `default` the field declares comes back with the option's own ([#5368](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5368)).
Those defaults hold no key set to `undefined`, so a `default` an ancestor declares for the field fills the keys they leave, as the form fills any other.
With `{ type: 'object', oneOf: [{ title: 'Obj', type: 'object', required: ['kind'], properties: { kind: { const: 'obj' }, note: { type: 'string', default: 'from-option' }, extra: { type: 'string' } } }, { title: 'None', type: 'null' }], default: { kind: 'obj', extra: 'from-root' } }`, select `None`, then `Obj`. Before: `{ note: 'from-option' }`. After: `{ kind: 'obj', note: 'from-option', extra: 'from-root' }`, the data the form mounted with.
With the same `oneOf` as `properties.w.properties.p` and the `default` declared on the root as `{ w: { p: { kind: 'obj', extra: 'from-root' } } }`, the round trip gave `{ w: { p: { note: 'from-option' } } }` and gives `{ w: { p: { kind: 'obj', note: 'from-option', extra: 'from-root' } } }`. The form mounts with `{ w: { p: { kind: 'obj', extra: 'from-root' } } }`, before and after: on mount the ancestor's `default` object stands in for the option's own leaf defaults, while the switch writes the option's defaults and the ancestor's fill the keys they leave.
An object property of the option gets the empty object a switch from another object option gives it, as before: with `meta: { type: 'object', properties: { a: { type: 'string' } } }` in `Obj`, the switch from `None` writes `meta: {}` before and after.

#### Cycle expansion is form state BREAKING CHANGE

`CyclicSchemaField` reads which fields are expanded from the form and renders an expanded one from `expandRefCycle(schema, rootSchema)`, which `@rjsf/utils` exports.
`retrieveSchema()` no longer reads `RJSF_REF_CYCLE_KEY: false` on a copy of a flagged schema as an expansion: the copy is resolved like any other copy of the field it was made from, keeping its `$ref` path, so its recursion collapses where the field's would.
A custom field that wrote `{ ...schema, [RJSF_REF_CYCLE_KEY]: false }` to expand a field calls `expandRefCycle()` instead.

- With `t: { $ref: node }` and `node = { type: 'object', properties: { name: { type: 'string' }, child: { $ref: node } } }`, click `#root_t_child-button`, then render the form with `t: { 'ui:field': Nothing }` and again without it. Before: `#root_t_child-button` again. After: `root_t_child_name` and `#root_t_child_child-button`, as before the remount.

### Sanitize is a diff of the field trees BREAKING CHANGE

`Form` sanitizes its data by comparing the field tree the data before a change builds with the one the data after it builds, field by field, as each is rendered: the option the form selects and the `$ref` cycles the user expanded.
A field that left the tree loses its value.
A field whose constraints changed (its `type`, `const`, `enum` or constant options, `maxItems`, or the `default` its own schema or its parent's `default` object declares) has its value judged again: a value still holding the old `default` takes the new one, written in place, and a value of another type, or one the new `const` or `enum` rejects, is replaced or dropped as `sanitizeDataForNewSchema()` does it.
A field whose constraints did not change keeps its value, whatever else the change did.
"Before" is what 6.11.0 gives.

- A condition inside the selected option replaces its old default ([#5465](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5465)). With `cfgFor = (name) => ({ type: 'object', default: { name }, properties: { name: { type: 'string' } } })`, `thing: { oneOf: [X, Y] }` where `X` is `{ type: 'object', properties: { mode: { enum: ['a', 'b'], default: 'a' } }, dependencies: { mode: { oneOf: [{ properties: { mode: { const: 'a' }, cfg: cfgFor('a') } }, { properties: { mode: { const: 'b' }, cfg: cfgFor('b') } }] } } }`, and the data `{ thing: { mode: 'a', cfg: { name: 'a' } } }`, select `b` for `mode`. Before: `cfg: { name: 'a' }`. After: `cfg: { name: 'b' }`.
- A default declared by a branch that requires the key is replaced too. With `cfg` declared plainly and `allOf: [{ if: { properties: { mode: { const: 'a' } }, required: ['mode', 'cfg'] }, then: { properties: { cfg: cfgFor('a') } } }, ...the same for 'b']`, select `b` for `mode` with the data `{ mode: 'a', cfg: { name: 'a' } }`. Before: `cfg: { name: 'a' }`. After: `cfg: { name: 'b' }`.
- An array or object write is sanitized like any other edit. With `list: { type: 'array', items: { type: 'string' } }`, `color: { enum: ['r', 'g'] }` and `allOf: [{ if: { properties: { list: { minItems: 2 } } }, then: { properties: { color: { enum: ['r'] } } } }]`, add a row to `list` with the data `{ color: 'g', list: ['x'] }`. Before: `color: 'g'` until the next edit replaced it. After: `color: 'r'` as the row is added.
- A schema change of a form that owns its data is sanitized as it arrives, unless the form is `disabled` or `readonly`, whose data no edit sanitizes either. With `color: { $ref: '#/definitions/Color' }` and `Color` narrowed from `enum: ['a', 'b']` to `enum: ['a']`, and `initialFormData` `{ color: 'b' }`. Before: `color: 'b'`, kept until an edit sanitized and, with no conditional in the schema, for good. After: `color: 'a'` at once. Whether 6.11.0 ever replaced the value turned on a conditional elsewhere in the schema, so the form now applies one rule, the one an edit follows, to the schema change itself.
- A value `setFieldValue('', data)` sets is taken as written, and no edit judges it unless the edit changes that field's constraints. With `mode: { enum: ['basic', 'advanced'] }`, `color: { type: 'string' }`, `note: { type: 'string' }` and `allOf: [{ if: { properties: { mode: { const: 'advanced' } } }, then: { properties: { color: { enum: ['r', 'c'] } } }, else: { properties: { color: { enum: ['r'] } } } }]`, set `{ mode: 'basic', color: 'c' }` and type into `note`: `color: 'c'` stays, which validation reports. An array `setFieldValue()` writes whole at its own path pairs each item that holds what an item of the array held with that item, so a reorder set back keeps the constraints its rows had.
- An extra key holding the old branch's `additionalProperties` default takes the new one, as a declared property does. With `mode: { enum: ['a', 'b'], default: 'a' }` and `dependencies: { mode: { oneOf: [{ properties: { mode: { const: 'a' } }, additionalProperties: tagFor('a') }, { properties: { mode: { const: 'b' } }, additionalProperties: tagFor('b') }] } }`, where `tagFor = (tag) => ({ type: 'object', default: { tag }, properties: { tag: { type: 'string' } } })`, and the data `{ mode: 'a', extra: { tag: 'a' } }`, select `b` for `mode`. Before: the form threw `Cannot use 'in' operator to search for 'tag' in a` as it rendered. After: `extra: { tag: 'b' }`.

`FormState` loses the members the previous sanitize compared against (`formDataSchema`, `settledFormData`, `settledFormDataForPairing`, `settledRetrievedSchema`) and `hasNestedConditionalSchema`, and gains `uiState`.
`sanitizeDataForNewSchema()` takes `newSchema`, `oldSchema` and `data` alone, as on 6.11.0: the `oldData`, `resolvedOldSchema` and `oldDataForPairing` parameters earlier v7 builds added are removed. An object property whose data holds no object is sanitized to an empty object, or left out where its schema declares no `properties`, rather than sanitized as an empty object, which filled the new defaults of its own properties and descended into their object properties in turn, without end behind a recursive `$ref`. The defaults the form computes fill those properties in.

#### `ui:required` and omitting extra data read the option on screen

The `ui:required` check and omitting extra data read the option a field shows and the cycles the user expanded, through a new optional `fieldState` argument of `getUiRequiredErrorSchema()` and `omitExtraData()`, which `Form` passes.
With `thing: { anyOf: [{ properties: { aField } }, { properties: { bField } }] }`, `ui:required` on `bField` and the data `{ thing: { aField: 'a' } }`, a field showing the second option now blocks the submit for the missing `bField`, where the check read the first option, which the data matches best.
With a recursive `node` whose `child` is `{ $ref: node }`, `ui:required` on `child.name` blocks the submit once the user expands `child`, and not before.
Omitting keeps the data of the option shown: with `thing: { anyOf: [{ properties: { a } }, { properties: { b } }] }`, the data `{ thing: { b: 'v' } }` and the first option shown, submitting with `omitExtraData` drops `b`, which the option on screen has no field for; it kept `b` while the option was read from the data.
An edit reads the option the field shows while it is made, so live omitting and live validation agree with the screen: with the root `oneOf: [{ title: 'A', properties: { a: { type: 'string' } } }, { title: 'B', properties: { a: { type: 'string', maxLength: 2 }, b: { type: 'string' } } }]`, `omitExtraData` and `liveOmit: 'onChange'`, select `B`, type `z` into `root_b` and `abc` into `root_a`. Before: the selector went to `A`, which the data now matches, and `b` was dropped with `root_b`. After: `B` stays on screen with `root_b`, and the data is `{ a: 'abc', b: 'z' }`. With `ui:required` on `b` and `liveValidate: 'onChange'` in place of the omit, the same steps report `.b must have required property 'b'` under `B`, where the form showed `A` and no error.
Omitting keeps its two policies, as on 6.11.0: an optional object whose filtered content is all empty is pruned, and an `anyOf` over empty data applies every branch.

### A field is named once per data path BREAKING CHANGE

A field's own `ui:field` and the schema's `$id` name a custom field through one slot, read in that order, so a field named by `$id` renders the way one named by `ui:field` does ([#5391](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5391)).
A named field is applied once per data path ([#5377](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5377)): a `SchemaField` rendered for the same data below it, and the selected `anyOf`/`oneOf` option, render as though no field were named.
"Before" is what 6.11.0 gives. With `fields={{ MyField }}` and `anyOf = { type: 'string', anyOf: [{ minLength: 2 }, { maxLength: 5 }] }`:

- `schema: { $id: 'MyField', ...anyOf }`. Before: `MyField` is not rendered; the option selector and the selected option's input render. After: `MyField` renders beside the option selector, above the selected option's input.
- `schema: { $id: 'MyField', ...anyOf }` with `uiSchema: { 'ui:fieldReplacesAnyOrOneOf': true }`. Before: as above. After: `MyField` renders alone, in place of the options.
- `schema: anyOf` with `uiSchema: { 'ui:field': 'MyField' }`. Before: `MyField` renders twice, beside the option selector and again in place of the selected option's input. After: once beside the option selector, and the selected option renders its own input ([#5358](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5358)). The field's own label is still left to `MyField`, as a `ui:field` always left it.
- `schema: anyOf` with `uiSchema: { 'ui:field': 'MyField', anyOf: [{ 'ui:field': 'OptionField' }, {}] }` and `OptionField` registered too. Before: `MyField` beside the option selector, and `OptionField` for the selected option. After: `MyField` beside the option selector, and the selected option's own input: the option renders at the data path `MyField` was named for, so its own `ui:field` is not applied, and the console warns once that it is ignored. A `ui:field` from a `ui:definitions` entry for the option's `$ref` is ignored the same way, and the warning names `uiSchema.anyOf[0]`, the entry the definition is copied into. The same holds for an option naming `OptionField` by `$id` (`anyOf: [{ $id: 'OptionField', minLength: 2 }, { maxLength: 5 }]`), which 6.11.0 rendered as `MyField` a second time, though a `$id`, an identifier first, is not warned about. Without the `ui:field` on the union, the option's own field renders, as before.
- `schema: { type: 'object', properties: { val: { type: 'string' } } }` with `uiSchema: { val: { 'ui:field': 'SchemaField' } }`. Before: the page runs out of memory. After: the field renders exactly as though no field were named, whichever of `ui:field`, `ui:options.field` or a `$id` names it: one input, `root_val`, labelled by the field's title, with one description, help and option selector. A field named `FallbackField` likewise renders as it does for a schema whose type sends it there, labelled as though no field were named.

A custom field that renders `registry.fields.SchemaField` for its own data gets the default field for the schema, whatever the `uiSchema` it passes names. That field draws the label, description and help the custom field's plan hands down to it, as [the plan section](#a-fields-label-description-help-and-errors-are-drawn-where-its-plan-says-breaking-change) describes, and the extra key's input and remove button and the `anyOf`/`oneOf` option selector stay with the field's own `SchemaField`, so each renders once. To render another custom field for the same data, render that field's component directly; to render a different custom field for an option, name it on the option alone, rather than beside a field named for the union.
Each `Form` starts with no field named, so a `Form` rendered inside a custom field applies its own root `ui:field` or `$id`.

### `getUiOptions()` returns a field's options alone BREAKING CHANGE

`getUiOptions(uiSchema, globalOptions)` leaves a `required` in `globalOptions` out, and no longer returns the root uiSchema's `ui:definitions` and `ui:globalOptions` keys as options ([#5527](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5527)).
A form-wide `required` was never read by the required marker or by validation, which take `ui:required` from the field's own uiSchema, but it reached the `options` of a boolean field's widget, of an array field's select, checkboxes or file widget, and of the `anyOf`/`oneOf` selector.
"Before" is what 6.11.0 gives.

- `schema: { type: 'object', properties: { flag: { type: 'boolean' } } }` with `uiSchema: { 'ui:globalOptions': { required: true } }`: the `CheckboxWidget`'s `options.required`. Before: `true`. After: `undefined`.
- `schema: { type: 'string' }` with `uiSchema: { 'ui:definitions': {} }`: the `TextWidget`'s `options.definitions`. Before: `{}`. After: `undefined`.

A field's own `ui:required` is still an option. Read the form's definitions and global options from `registry.uiSchemaDefinitions` and `registry.globalUiOptions`.

### A custom field rendering `FallbackField` without a plan has the value field draw its label BREAKING CHANGE

Under [`useFallbackUiForUnsupportedType`](../api-reference/form-props.md#usefallbackuiforunsupportedtype), a custom field that renders `registry.fields.FallbackField` without handing it its `presentation` has the value field, below the type selector, draw the field's label and description, however the form reached the custom field ([#5389](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5389)).
Without a plan, `FallbackField` can't tell whether the custom field draws a label of its own, so it always has the value field draw one, which a custom field drawing none needs to give its value an accessible name.
"Before" is what 6.11.0 gives. With `schema: { type: 'object', properties: { val: { type: ['string', 'number'], title: 'Val', description: 'Desc' } } }` and a custom field `MyField` that draws no label or description of its own:

- `MyField` named by `ui:field` or `ui:options.field`. Before: the page runs out of stack, since `FallbackField` rendered its value through `SchemaField` with the `uiSchema` that names `MyField`, which [is now applied once per data path](#a-field-is-named-once-per-data-path-breaking-change). After: one label, `Val`, below the type selector, and one description.
- `MyField` named by the schema's `$id`. Before: two labels, `Val` and `Value`. After: as above.
- `MyField` named by a `ui:field` that it removes from the `uiSchema` it passes on. Before: one label, `Value`, and no description. After: as above.

A custom field that draws its own label and description gets both a second time on every one of those routes, the second description under the same `id` as its own.
Leave the label and description to the value field: draw neither in the custom field, and the value field draws each once.

### `ui:globalOptions` hold only the options RJSF reads there BREAKING CHANGE

RJSF reads twelve options from the root uiSchema's `ui:globalOptions`, as fallbacks for a field that sets none of its own: `addable`, `copyable`, `orderable`, `removable`, `label`, `allowClearTextInputs`, `duplicateKeySuffixSeparator`, `enableMarkdownInDescription`, `enableMarkdownInHelp`, `enableOptionalDataFieldForType`, `optionValueFormat` and `deprecatedHandling` ([#5607](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/5607)).
They are the `GlobalUISchemaOptionsKeys` type, and the `GLOBAL_UI_OPTIONS_KEYS` constant lists them.
Every other option is read per field only: the `Form` builds its registry's `globalUiOptions` with [`pickGlobalUiOptions()`](../api-reference/utility-functions.md#pickglobaluioptions), which keeps those twelve and the extension keys the form declares, and nothing else.
The console warns once about each option RJSF reads per field that `ui:globalOptions` still sets, such as `readonly` or `widget`.
A custom component hands [`getUiOptions()`](../api-reference/utility-functions.md#getuioptions) the registry's `globalUiOptions`, which hold the extension keys the theme and the `Form` declare. One that reads a root uiSchema's `ui:globalOptions` of its own instead passes them through `pickGlobalUiOptions()` with those keys, as its second argument, or it keeps only RJSF's twelve.
An option set globally that RJSF no longer reads there moves as follows:

| Set in `ui:globalOptions`                                                                                                           | Set instead                                                                                                                                                                                |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| `widget`, `field`                                                                                                                   | the field's own `ui:widget` or `ui:field`, or a replacement for the registered implementation through the `Form`'s `widgets` or `fields`, such as `widgets={{ TextWidget: MyTextWidget }}` |
| A template override, such as `FieldTemplate`                                                                                        | the `Form`'s `templates`                                                                                                                                                                   |
| `readonly`, `disabled`                                                                                                              | the `Form`'s `readonly` and `disabled` props                                                                                                                                               |
| `hideError`                                                                                                                         | `ui:hideError` at the root of the uiSchema, which reaches every field below it                                                                                                             |
| `help`, `title`, `description`, `placeholder`, `classNames`, `inputType`, an array's `itemLabel` and any other option a field reads | the field's own uiSchema                                                                                                                                                                   |

`GlobalUISchemaOptions` is closed to those twelve keys, so a `ui:globalOptions` literal with any other key no longer compiles.
Its `UserExtensions` parameter adds the keys your own components read, which may not reuse an option name RJSF reads for a field, as `ReservedUiOptionName` and `RESERVED_UI_OPTION_NAMES` list them.
Declare the options against it, hand them to the uiSchema, which takes them as the plain type, and name their keys in the `Form`'s new [`globalUiOptionsKeys`](../api-reference/form-props.md#globaluioptionskeys) prop:

```ts
import { GlobalUISchemaOptions, UiSchema } from '@rjsf/utils';

const globalOptions: GlobalUISchemaOptions<{ density?: 'compact' | 'comfortable' }> = {
  label: false,
  density: 'compact',
};
const uiSchema: UiSchema = { 'ui:globalOptions': globalOptions };

<Form schema={schema} uiSchema={uiSchema} validator={validator} globalUiOptionsKeys={['density']} />;
```

Every field then hands the declared keys to its widget in its `options`, beneath the field's own, as it does RJSF's, a template reads them with `getUiOptions(uiSchema, registry.globalUiOptions)`, and [`getGlobalUiOptions<MyExtensions>(registry)`](../api-reference/utility-functions.md#getglobaluioptions) reads them off the registry typed with the extension.
A key that is an option name RJSF reads per field, such as `widget`, is not read from `ui:globalOptions`, and a warning says so.

The themes that read options of their own globally export a type for them, which takes your `UserExtensions` too: `@rjsf/antd`'s `AntdGlobalUISchemaOptions` (`block`), `@rjsf/chakra-ui`'s `ChakraGlobalUISchemaOptions` (`chakra`), `@rjsf/daisyui`'s `DaisyGlobalUISchemaOptions` (`daisy`), `@rjsf/mantine`'s `MantineGlobalUISchemaOptions` (`gridCols`, `gridSpacing` and `gridVerticalSpacing`) and `@rjsf/mui`'s `MuiGlobalUISchemaOptions` (`mui`).
Each of them also exports a `UiSchema` type whose `ui:globalOptions` carry its own option and your `UserExtensions`, so the option compiles there inline, such as `{ 'ui:globalOptions': { mui: { size: 'small' } } }` typed by `@rjsf/mui`'s `UiSchema`.
Each declares its keys as its theme's `globalUiOptionsKeys`, a new `ThemeProps` field that `withTheme()` adds the `Form`'s to, so a custom widget reads the theme's option from its `options`, and a custom template with `getUiOptions(uiSchema, registry.globalUiOptions)`, as the theme's own do. Any other theme option is read per field only, unless the `Form` declares it in `globalUiOptionsKeys`.
A theme's type also rejects a `UserExtensions` key its widgets read as an option of their own: `@rjsf/daisyui`'s `multiple`, `color`, `size` and `colorGradient`, and `@rjsf/mantine`'s `wrapperProps`, `thumbProps`, `thumbLabel`, `valueFormat`, `displayFormat` and the `InputWrapper` options, such as `size`, `labelProps` and `inputContainer`.
`@rjsf/shadcn` exports no such type, but its `RangeWidget` reads `props` from its options and folds it into the slider's, so don't name an extension `props` with it.
A `Form` from `@rjsf/core` handed a theme's `widgets` and `templates` directly, rather than built with `withTheme()` or the theme's own `Form`, declares none of the theme's keys, so it reads the theme's option from `ui:globalOptions` only when its `globalUiOptionsKeys` name it.

:::warning

`@rjsf/mantine`'s widgets hand the options they don't recognize to their Mantine component as props, but leave out every key the registry's `globalUiOptions` hold that the field doesn't set itself, so a key declared for your own components, such as `density`, never reaches a Mantine input, while a field's own Mantine prop of the same name still does.
A Mantine prop set in `ui:globalOptions`, such as `radius` or `variant`, reached the Mantine components of `BooleanField`'s and `ArrayField`'s widgets before, and now reaches none, even when declared: set it in each field's `ui:options` instead, or as a component's `defaultProps` in Mantine's own theme.

:::

`StringField`, and `NumberField` through it, hand their widget the global options beneath the field's own, as `ArrayField` and `BooleanField` already did, so a global `allowClearTextInputs` or `optionValueFormat` now reaches every string and number widget.

`LayoutGridField` no longer copies the globals into each cell's uiSchema, which gave a cell's components globals no field outside a grid got.
A cell's `ui:options` hold its own options and the `ui:layoutGrid` entry's props, and the cell carries no `ui:globalOptions`, so its widget's `options` no longer hold a `globalOptions` key.
A custom field, widget or template in a cell that read a global from its `uiSchema` reads `registry.globalUiOptions` instead, as one outside a grid does, or `getUiOptions(uiSchema, registry.globalUiOptions)`.
A cell's own option, given as `ui:<name>` or in `ui:options`, now beats the global one, where the copy let a global `label: false` hide the label of a cell that set `ui:label: true`.
Only the root uiSchema's `ui:globalOptions` are read, so one set on a nested grid's uiSchema no longer applies to that grid's cells.

## New Features

### `getFormData()` and the `FormRef` type

The form owns its data, the way an `<input>` with a `defaultValue` does (see [`Form` owns its data](#form-owns-its-data-breaking-change)), and until now the only way to see it was `onChange`, so autosave, an "unsaved changes" guard, or a submit button rendered outside the form all had to copy every change into your own state.
`getFormData()` reads it on demand:

```tsx
function Editor({ record }) {
  const formRef = useRef<FormRef>(null);

  useEffect(() => {
    const timer = setInterval(() => api.draft(formRef.current?.getFormData()), 30_000);
    return () => clearInterval(timer);
  }, []);

  return <Form ref={formRef} schema={schema} validator={validator} initialFormData={record} />;
}
```

It returns the form's current value: the seed, filled in with the schema's defaults, and every edit made since, including one React has yet to show in the inputs.

`FormRef<T>` names every supported ref member. The ref section above shows how to type a ref against it.

### `transformChange`

A pure `(next, previous) => data` prop that decides what an edit leaves the form with: `next` takes the edit, `previous` refuses it, and any other value rewrites it, before the form stores, validates and reports the result. It replaces refusing an edit by not storing it, which no longer works now that the form owns its data. See [Refuse or rewrite an edit in `transformChange`](#refuse-or-rewrite-an-edit-in-transformchange).

### `ui:initialValue` and `ui:required`

Two new uiSchema directives complement the extended `ui:emptyValue` above, for forms that want to control a field's initial value or required-ness without editing the underlying schema:

- **`ui:initialValue`** pre-fills a field on initial render and after a form reset. It takes priority over `schema.default`, but never overrides `formData` that's already been provided. This is useful for a field — often hidden — that a particular form wants to fix to a known value, e.g. a `country` field defaulted to `'US'` for a US-only form.
- **`ui:required`** overrides a field's `required` status from the UI side only. Setting it to `true` shows the required indicator and adds the field to the effective required set used for validation, even if the schema doesn't mark it required. Setting it to `false` hides the indicator on a schema-required field, but does **not** suppress schema-level validation — pair it with `ui:initialValue` or `ui:emptyValue` so the field always has a value, or you'll get a `console.warn`. `ui:required` must be set per field; it is not honored via `ui:globalOptions`.

See [`ui:initialValue`](../api-reference/uiSchema.md#initialvalue) and [`ui:required`](../api-reference/uiSchema.md#required) for details and examples.

`ui:required` is enforced by walking the rendered form itself rather than by rewriting the schema handed to your validator, so it works the same way regardless of which validator you use — including the precompiled validators (`AJV8PrecompiledValidator` from `@rjsf/validator-ajv8` and `ATAPrecompiledValidator` from `@rjsf/validator-ata`), which enforce it too. The one difference from a schema-level required error: a `ui:required` error is built by RJSF, not your validator, so it doesn't pass through a custom `transformErrors` function and doesn't carry validator-specific fields like AJV's `params.missingProperty`. See [`ui:required`](../api-reference/uiSchema.md#required) for details.

### `ui:options.optgroups` for grouped `<select>` options

Every theme's `SelectWidget` now supports grouping enum options into labeled sections (rendered as `<optgroup>` elements, or each theme's closest equivalent — see the [`optgroups` docs](../api-reference/uiSchema.md#optgroups) for the specifics per theme) via `ui:options.optgroups`:

```tsx
const uiSchema: UiSchema = {
  'ui:options': {
    optgroups: {
      Latin: ['lorem', 'ipsum', 'dolorem'],
      Greek: ['alpha', 'beta', 'gamma'],
    },
  },
};
```

Keys are the group labels; values are arrays of enum values belonging to that group. Enum values not listed in any group render ungrouped after the groups. This is purely additive — existing forms are unaffected unless `optgroups` is set.

### `propertyNames.enum` renders the key as a dropdown

An object whose `propertyNames` enumerates the allowed key names now renders each `additionalProperties`/`patternProperties` key as a dropdown of those names rather than a free-text input, in every theme:

```tsx
const schema: RJSFSchema = {
  type: 'object',
  additionalProperties: { type: 'boolean' },
  propertyNames: { enum: ['darkMode', 'betaBanner', 'offlineCache'] },
};
```

A name another key already holds is left out of the dropdown, and the add button creates the new property under the first allowed name that is still free instead of `newKey`.
`canExpand()` hides the add button once every allowed name is taken, the way it already does at the `maxProperties` limit, so a **custom `ObjectFieldTemplate`** that calls it picks this up on its own.
A `propertyNames` without an `enum`, such as one constraining names by `pattern`, keeps the free-text input, so existing forms are unaffected.

The names reach `WrapIfAdditionalTemplate` as a new optional `propertyNamesEnum` prop on `FieldTemplateProps`/`WrapIfAdditionalTemplateProps`.
A **custom `FieldTemplate`** that lists the props it forwards to `WrapIfAdditionalTemplate` one by one, rather than spreading them, has to add `propertyNamesEnum` to that list for the dropdown to appear, and `keyName` for the key it renders to be the real one (see [`FieldTemplateProps` gains a required `keyName`](#fieldtemplateprops-gains-a-required-keyname-breaking-change)).
A **custom `WrapIfAdditionalTemplate`** renders the dropdown by handing the prop to the new `AdditionalPropertyKeySelect` component from `@rjsf/utils`, which resolves the theme's own `SelectWidget`:

```tsx
{propertyNamesEnum ? (
  <AdditionalPropertyKeySelect
    id={`${id}-key`}
    label={keyLabel}
    value={keyName}
    propertyNamesEnum={propertyNamesEnum}
    onKeyRename={onKeyRename}
    disabled={disabled}
    readonly={readonly}
    required={required}
    registry={registry}
  />
) : (
  <input type='text' id={`${id}-key`} onBlur={onKeyRenameBlur} defaultValue={keyName} />
)}
```

### Multiple types and unconstrained properties can be edited through the fallback UI

JSON Schema lets a `type` be a list of types.
v6 rendered the first type in that list and gave no way to reach the others, and it rendered a property described only by `additionalProperties: true` as a field for whatever type the data happened to hold, with the same one-way door.
Turning on the [useFallbackUiForUnsupportedType](../api-reference/form-props.md#usefallbackuiforunsupportedtype) prop now renders both through `FallbackField`, which pairs a type selector with the field for the type currently chosen:

```tsx
const schema: RJSFSchema = {
  type: 'object',
  properties: {
    // a selector offering exactly these three types
    multi: { type: ['string', 'number', 'boolean'] },
    // a selector offering every JSON Schema type, for each key added here
    anything: { type: 'object', additionalProperties: true },
  },
};

render(<Form schema={schema} validator={validator} useFallbackUiForUnsupportedType />, document.getElementById('app'));
```

Switching the type casts the value that is already there where it can (a number to its string spelling, a string to the number it reads as) and starts from an empty value where it cannot, such as an object, an array, or a number from text that reads as no number at all.
The selector also now offers `integer` and `null` alongside the five types it offered in v6, and starts on the type the current form data has.
Every other keyword of the schema still describes the value, so a union declaring `properties`, `items`, `format` or `minimum` keeps them for whichever type is in effect.

A schema pairing multiple types with an `anyOf` or `oneOf` gets both selectors: the type selector wraps the option selector, and each option renders for whichever type is currently chosen.
Every member of the union is therefore reachable from within an option, where in v6 an option could only ever be the first type in the list.

Four things are deliberately unchanged:

- **A nullable type** (`['string', 'null']`) still renders as a plain `string` field, since [getSchemaType()](../api-reference/utility-functions.md#getschematype) resolves it to that single type. Only a union of more than one non-`null` type gets a selector.
- **With the prop off** (the default), there is still no selector, since it is the opt-in fallback UI, so a union renders one field for one of its types. That is the first type in the list that JSON Schema defines other than `null`, so `['null', 'string', 'number']` renders its `string` and `['foo', 'string']` its `string` rather than the unsupported-field box. A `ui:widget` that only another listed `string`, `number`, `integer` or `boolean` type has renders that type's field instead, so a `textarea` on a `['number', 'string']` renders `StringField` where v6 threw; a `format` picks no other type's field. A union paired with an `anyOf` or `oneOf` stays one-way too, since the option selector alone cannot reach the other types.
- **A schema pinning its value with an `enum`, a `const`, or an `anyOf`/`oneOf` of constants** renders as the select or constant field it always has, with no type selector, since switching type would only cast a value the user picked into one the schema rejects.
- **A field whose `ui:widget` is a component** renders through that control alone, with no type selector, the same way one with a `ui:field` does.
  A control written for that schema handles the types it allows, so wrapping it would pin the schema to one of them and cast the value on every switch.
  A `ui:widget` naming a widget by string is a theme's control for a single type, which is the choice the selector is there to make, so it renders within the selector instead — and is dropped for a chosen type the theme has no implementation of.
  A schema with no usable type of its own — an unrecognized `type`, or an `additionalProperties` entry left unconstrained — still gets a selector, since it lists no types for such a control to handle, and the widget renders within it for whichever type is chosen.
  A list naming an unrecognized type alongside a recognized one offers only the recognized ones, `null` included: a `['foo', 'null']` offers `null` and nothing else.

See [Multiple types](../json-schema/single.md#multiple-types) for details.

### `additionalPropertiesWhenAbsent` reads an object naming no `additionalProperties` as the spec does

JSON Schema reads an object that names neither `additionalProperties` nor `unevaluatedProperties` as taking any extra key, as `additionalProperties: true` does, where the form has always read it as taking none ([#2137](https://github.com/rjsf-team/react-jsonschema-form/issues/2137)).
The new [`additionalPropertiesWhenAbsent`](../api-reference/form-props.md#additionalpropertieswhenabsent) prop, `'forbid'` by default, has the form follow the spec when set to `'allow'`: such an object gets an add button, the extra keys of its data render with their key input, and `omitExtraData` keeps them.
An object `anyOf`/`oneOf` union then renders its own title and add button beside the option it shows; give it `additionalProperties: false` to keep it closed.
See [Objects naming no `additionalProperties`](../json-schema/objects.md#objects-naming-no-additionalproperties).

### New types

The following new types were added to `@rjsf/utils`:

- **`MarkdownTemplateProps<T, S, F>`**: the props a `MarkdownTemplate` receives, generic over the same `<T, S, F>` as every other template's props. `children` is the markdown string to render; `uiSchema` and `registry` are picked from the form's base props, so an implementation can read `ui:options`, reach `registry.translateString`, or fall back to another template.
- **`FieldPath`**: a branded string identifying a field by the path to its data, such as `friends[0].firstName`. See [Field identity](#field-identity-is-a-fieldpath-string-instead-of-a-fieldpathid-object-breaking-change).
- **`UiOptionsCheck`**: The `{ when, then }` shape a `Checks` union is built from, each rule declaring the widget/field/option vocabulary `StrictUiSchema` allows for a given form-data type.
- **`StrictUiSchema<Checks, T, S, F>`**: the opt-in form of `UiSchema` that narrows `ui:widget`/`ui:field`/`ui:options` to a `Checks` union's vocabulary. See [Type-safe UiSchema (opt-in)](#type-safe-uischema-opt-in).
